# Syria

Not to be confused with [Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria).

Several terms redirect here. For other uses, see [Syria (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(disambiguation\)) and [Syrian Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Republics).

**Syria**,[^cite_note-23] officially the **Syrian Arab Republic**,[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] is a country in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) located in the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant). It borders the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the west, [Turkey](11125639.md) to [the north and northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria%E2%80%93Turkey_border), [Iraq](7515928.md) to [the east and southeast](46975552.md), [Jordan](7515964.md) to [the south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan%E2%80%93Syria_border), and [Israel](9282173.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md) to [the southwest](46232625.md). It is a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) under [a provisional government](79584988.md) and comprises [14 governorates](690220.md). [Damascus](8914.md) is Syria's capital and largest city. With a population of 26 million across an area of 185,180 square kilometres (71,500 sq mi), it is the [56th-most](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) populous and [87th-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) country.&#x20;

The name "Syria" historically referred to the [region of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)), or known as Greater Syria and Syria-Palestine. The modern state encompasses the sites of several ancient kingdoms and empires, including the [Eblan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebla) civilization. Damascus was the capital of the [Umayyad Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) and later became an important political and administrative center, alongside [Aleppo](159244.md), under the [Ayyubid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty) and [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate) Sultanates and the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). The modern Syrian state was established in the mid-20th century [after centuries of Ottoman rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria), as a [French Mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_for_Syria_and_the_Lebanon). The state represented the largest Arab state to emerge from the formerly [Ottoman](22278.md)-ruled [Syrian provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria). It gained *de jure* independence as a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) in 1945 when the [First Syrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Syrian_Republic) became a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md), an act which legally ended the French Mandate. French troops [withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant_Crisis) in April 1946, granting the nation *de facto* independence. The post-independence period was tumultuous, with [multiple coups and coup attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Syrian_coups_d%27%C3%A9tat) between 1949 and 1971. In 1958, Syria entered a [brief pan-Arab union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic) with [Egypt](8087628.md), which was terminated following a [1961 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat). The [1963 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) carried out by the [military committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region#Military_Bureau) of the [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region) established a [one-party state](102459.md), which [ran Syria](57134800.md) under martial law from 1963 to 2011. Internal power-struggles within [Ba'athist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%27athism) factions caused further coups in [1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) and [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrective_Movement_\(Syria\)), the latter of which saw [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad) come to power. Under Assad, Syria became a [hereditary dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynasty). [Assad died](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Hafez_al-Assad) in 2000, and he was succeeded by his son, [Bashar](364813.md).&#x20;

During the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) in 2011, members of the [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) organized protests against the Assad regime, which became known as the [Syrian Revolution](39750126.md). After the government responded with lethal violence, the [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army) was formed, and the revolution escalated into a multifaceted [13-year civil war](30741795.md), drawing in several countries and triggering a [refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war) that displaced more than 6 million people. Rebel advances were reversed following the emergence of the [Islamic State](9087364.md), which occupied roughly two-thirds of Syria by May 2015. Alongside rebel and government counteroffensives, [several countries led a campaign](43517689.md) that resulted in the Islamic State's territorial defeat in 2017. Following its defeat in the four-year [Battle of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2012%E2%80%932016\)), the Free Syrian Army fragmented and declined.&#x20;

By 2020, the civil war had become a frozen conflict, while [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (HTS) consolidated the remaining rebel groups in its stronghold in northwestern Syria. In late 2024, HTS and other opposition forces launched [a series of offensives](78460726.md), culminating in the [fall of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)) and the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) on 8 December 2024. The [Syrian caretaker government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government) was established following the fall of the Assad regime under [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md), who became Syria's de facto [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_Syria) and was formally appointed president of Syria at the [Syrian Revolution Victory Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Revolution_Victory_Conference) on 29 January 2025. The caretaker government remained in place until it was replaced by the [Syrian transitional government](79584988.md) in March 2025. [Post-war conflict](81373641.md) has continued following the fall of the Assad regime, involving clashes with [Assad](36629941.md) loyalists, [Alawite](436616.md) and [Druze](47104446.md) insurgents, the former [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md), and Israeli forces.&#x20;

Syria is a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) presidential [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system) currently undergoing a political transition. It is governed by the Syrian transitional government under the [Constitutional Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Declaration_of_the_Syrian_Arab_Republic), with executive authority vested in the [president](516106.md), while the [People's Assembly](4504009.md) serves as the [unicameral](204304.md) legislature and [judicial power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary) is vested in an [independent judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence). Syria is a [developing](78449.md) country with a mixed economy. Its [economy](27476.md) was primarily based on agriculture in its early years but deteriorated following the outbreak of the Syrian civil war in 2011. A country of fertile plains, high mountains, plateaus, and deserts, Syria is home to [diverse ethnic and religious groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Syria). [Levantine Arabs](2185.md) are the largest ethnic group, while [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) constitute the largest religious group. Syria is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md) and the [Arab League](52625.md), and is a member of numerous [international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Syria). It is also home to six [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Syria).&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Syria)

Several sources indicate that the name *Syria* is borrowed from the [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) name Σύριοι *_Sýrioi_*, or Σύροι *_Sýroi_*, which is in turn borrowed from [Luwian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luwian_language) *sura/i-* (attested in the 8th century BC), and ultimately derived from Aššūr ([Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria)) in northern [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) (present-day Iraq and north-eastern Syria).[^cite_note-Rollinger-26][^cite_note-27] However, from the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) (323–150 BC), this term was also applied to the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant),[^cite_note-28] and from this point the Greeks applied the term without distinction between the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people) of Mesopotamia and [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) of the Levant.[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30] Mainstream modern academic opinion strongly favors the argument that the Greek word is related to Ἀσσυρία *_[Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria)_*, ultimately borrowed from [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) *_[Aššur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assur)_*.[^cite_note-31] The Greek name appears to correspond to [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_language) *_ʾšr_* 'Assur', *_ʾšrym_* 'Assyrians', recorded in the [Çineköy inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87inek%C3%B6y_inscription) (dated to the 8th century BC), where Luwian *sura/i-* is also attested.[^cite_note-Rollinger-26]&#x20;

The area designated by the word has changed over time. Classically, Syria lies at the eastern end of the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean), between [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia) to the south and [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) to the north, stretching inland to include parts of Iraq, and having an uncertain border to the northeast that [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) describes as including, from west to east, [Commagene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commagene), [Sophene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophene), and [Adiabene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabene).[^cite_note-32]&#x20;

By Pliny's time, however, this larger Syria had been divided into a number of provinces under the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) (but politically independent from each other): [Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)), later renamed [Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) in AD 135 (the region corresponding to modern-day Israel, the Palestinian territories, and Jordan) in the extreme southwest; [Phoenice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenice_\(Roman_province\)) (established in 194) corresponding to modern Lebanon, Damascus and Homs regions; [Coele-Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coele-Syria) (or "Hollow Syria") and south of the [Eleutheris river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahr_al-Kabir).[^cite_note-33]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Syria)

### Ancient antiquity

Reconstructed sculpture of a bird from [Tell Halaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_Halaf)



[Ishqi-Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishqi-Mari), king of the Second Kingdom of [Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari,_Syria), circa 2300 BC

The [Natufian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natufian_culture) was the first to become sedentary around the 11th millennium BC[^cite_note-34] and became one of the centers of [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) culture (known as [Pre-Pottery Neolithic A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PPNA)), where agriculture and cattle breeding first began to appear. The site of [Tell Qaramel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_Qaramel) has several round stone towers dated to 10650 BC, making them the oldest structures of this kind in the world.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] The Neolithic period ([Pre-Pottery Neolithic B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pottery_Neolithic_B)) is represented by rectangular houses of [Mureybet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mureybet) culture. At the time, people used containers made of stone, gyps, and burnt lime ([Vaisselle blanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Ware)). The discovery of [obsidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obsidian) tools from [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) are evidence of early trade. The ancient cities of [Hamoukar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamoukar) and [Emar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emar) played an important role during the late Neolithic and Bronze Age. Archaeologists have demonstrated that civilization in Syria was one of the most ancient on earth, perhaps preceded by only that of [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia).&#x20;

The earliest recorded indigenous civilization in the region was the Kingdom of [Ebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebla)[^cite_note-37] near present-day [Idlib](2528679.md), northern Syria. Ebla appears to have been founded around 3500 BC[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] and gradually built its fortune through trade with the Mesopotamian states of [Sumer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer), [Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria), and [Akkad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkad_\(city\)), as well as with the [Hurrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurrian) and [Hattian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattians) peoples to the northwest, in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor).[^cite_note-LibraryofCongress-43] Gifts from [Pharaohs](23294.md), found during excavations, confirm Ebla's contact with [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt). One of the earliest written texts from Syria is a trading agreement between Vizier [Ibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrium) of Ebla and an ambiguous kingdom called [Abarsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abarsal) c. 2300 BC. This is known as the [Treaty between Ebla and Abarsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_between_Ebla_and_Abarsal).[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45] Scholars believe the [language of Ebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eblaite_language) to be among the oldest known written [Semitic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages) after [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language). Recent classifications of the Eblaite language have shown that it was an [East Semitic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Semitic_language), closely related to the [Akkadian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language).[^cite_note-46] Ebla was weakened by a long war with [Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari,_Syria), and the whole of Syria became part of the Mesopotamian [Akkadian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire) after [Sargon of Akkad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_of_Akkad) and his grandson [Naram-Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naram-Sin_of_Akkad)'s conquests ended Eblan domination over Syria in the first half of the 23rd century BC.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] The [Yazidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidi) people have also lived in modern-day Syria since ancient times.[^cite_note-49]&#x20;

By the 21st century BC, Hurrians settled in the northern east parts of Syria while the rest of the region was dominated by the [Amorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amorites). Syria was called the Land of the Amurru (Amorites) by their Assyro-Babylonian neighbors. The Northwest Semitic [Amorite language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amorite_language) is the earliest attested of the [Canaanite languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_languages). [Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari,_Syria) reemerged during this period until conquered by [Hammurabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammurabi) of Babylon. [Ugarit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugarit) also arose during this time, circa 1800 BC, close to modern [Latakia](195594.md). [Ugaritic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugaritic_language) was a Semitic language loosely related to the Canaanite languages and developed the [Ugaritic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugaritic_alphabet),[^cite_note-50] considered to be the world's earliest known alphabet. The Ugaritic kingdom survived until its destruction at the hands of the marauding Indo-European [Sea Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Peoples) in the 12th century BC in what was known as the [Late Bronze Age collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_collapse).&#x20;

[Aleppo](159244.md) and Damascus are among the [oldest continuously inhabited cities in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_continuously_inhabited_cities).[^cite_note-51] [Yamhad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamhad) (modern Aleppo) dominated northern Syria for two centuries,[^cite_note-google44-52] although eastern Syria was occupied in the 19th and 18th centuries BC by the [Old Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Assyrian_Empire) ruled by the Amorite dynasty of [Shamshi-Adad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamshi-Adad_I), and by the [Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_Empire) which was founded by Amorites. Yamhad was described in the tablets of Mari as the mightiest state in the near east and as having more vassals than Hammurabi.[^cite_note-google44-52] Yamhad imposed its authority over [Alalakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alalakh),[^cite_note-53] [Qatna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatna),[^cite_note-54] the Hurrians states, and the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates) valley down to the borders with Babylon.[^cite_note-55] The army of Yamhad campaigned as far away as [Dēr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_\(Sumer\)) on the border of [Elam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elam) (modern Iran).[^cite_note-56] Yamhad was conquered and destroyed, along with Ebla, by the [Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites) from [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) circa 1600 BC.[^cite_note-57] From this time, Syria became a battle ground for various foreign empires, these being the [Hittite Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittite_Empire), [Mitanni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitanni) Empire, [Egyptian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Empire), [Middle Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Assyrian_Empire), and to a lesser degree [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia). The Egyptians initially occupied much of the south, while the Hittites and the Mitanni occupied much of the north. However, Assyria eventually gained the upper hand, destroying the Mitanni Empire and annexing huge swathes of territory previously held by the Hittites and Babylon.&#x20;





Syrians bringing presents to Pharaoh [Tuthmosis III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuthmosis_III), as depicted in the tomb of [Rekhmire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rekhmire), circa 1450 BCE (actual painting and interpretational drawing). They are labeled "Chiefs of [Retjenu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retjenu)".

[^cite_note-58]

[^cite_note-59]

Around the 14th century BC, various [Semitic people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_people) appeared in the area, such as the semi-nomadic [Suteans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suteans) who came into an unsuccessful conflict with Babylonia to the east, and the [West Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Semitic_languages) speaking [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) who subsumed the earlier Amorites. They too were subjugated by Assyria and the Hittites for centuries. The Egyptians fought the Hittites for control over western Syria; the fighting reached its zenith in 1274 BC with the [Battle of Kadesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kadesh).[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] The west remained part of the Hittite empire until its destruction c. 1200 BC,[^cite_note-62] while eastern Syria largely became part of the Middle Assyrian Empire,[^cite_note-63] who also annexed much of the west during the reign of [Tiglath-Pileser I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiglath-Pileser_I) 1114–1076 BC. With the destruction of the Hittites and the decline of Assyria in the late 11th century BC, the Aramean tribes gained control of much of the interior, founding states such as [Bit Bahiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bit_Bahiani), [Aram-Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram-Damascus), [Hamath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamath), [Aram-Rehob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram-Rehob), [Aram-Naharaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram-Naharaim), and [Luhuti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhuti). From this point, the region became known as [Aramea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramea) or [Aram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram_\(biblical_region\)). There was also a synthesis between the Semitic Arameans and the remnants of the Indo-European Hittites, with the founding of a number of [Syro-Hittite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syro-Hittite) states centered in north central Aram (Syria) and south central Asia Minor (modern Turkey), including [Palistin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palistin), [Carchemish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carchemish) and [Sam'al](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam%27al).&#x20;



[Amrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrit) Phoenician Temple

A [Canaanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_languages) group known as the [Phoenicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicians) came to dominate the coasts of Syria, (and also Lebanon and northern [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\))) from the 13th century BC, founding [city states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_states) such as [Amrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrit), [Simyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simyra), [Arwad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arwad), [Paltos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_al-Mulk), [Ramitha](195594.md#Ancient_settlement_and_founding), and [Shuksi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_Sukas).[^cite_note-64] From these coastal regions, they eventually spread their influence throughout the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean), including building colonies in [Malta](19137.md), Sicily, the [Iberian peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_peninsula), and the coasts of North Africa and most significantly, founding the major city-state of [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage) in the 9th century BC, which was much later to become the center of a major empire, rivaling the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic).&#x20;

Syria and the western half of [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East) then fell to the vast [Neo Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo_Assyrian_Empire) (911 BC – 605 BC). The Assyrians introduced [Imperial Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Aramaic) as the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) of their empire. This language was to remain dominant in Syria and the entire [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East) until after the [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic) conquest in the 7th and 8th centuries AD, and was to be a vehicle for the spread of Christianity.[^cite_note-65] The Assyrians named their colonies of Syria and Lebanon [Eber-Nari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eber-Nari).[^cite_note-66] Assyrian domination ended after the Assyrians greatly weakened themselves in a series of brutal internal civil wars, followed by attacks from: the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes), [Babylonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonians), [Chaldeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaldea), [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people), [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians) and [Cimmerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cimmerians). During the fall of Assyria, the [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians) ravaged and plundered much of Syria. The last stand of the Assyrian army was at [Carchemish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carchemish) in northern Syria in 605 BC. The Assyrian Empire was followed by the [Neo-Babylonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) (605 BC – 539 BC). During this period, Syria became a battle ground between Babylonia and another former Assyrian colony, that of Egypt. The Babylonians, like their Assyrian relations, were victorious over Egypt.&#x20;

### Classical antiquity

Main articles: [Eber-Nari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eber-Nari), [Coele-Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coele-Syria), [Roman Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Syria), and [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina)



[Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch) in Syria became the capital of the Hellenistic [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) in 240 BC

Lands that constitute modern-day Syria were part of the Neo-Babylonian Empire and had been annexed by the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) in 539 BC. Led by [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great), the Achaemenid Persians retained [Imperial Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Aramaic) as one of the diplomatic languages of their empire, as well as the Assyrian name for the new [satrapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satrapy) of Aram/Syria [Eber-Nari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eber-Nari). Syria was conquered by the [Macedonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Empire) which was ruled by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) c. 330 BC and consequently became [Coele-Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coele-Syria) province of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) (323 BC – 64 BC), with the Seleucid kings styling themselves "King of Syria" and the city of Antioch being its capital starting from 240 BC. Thus, it was the Greeks who introduced the name "Syria" to the region. Originally an Indo-European corruption of "Assyria" in northern Mesopotamia (Iraq), the Greeks used this term to describe not only Assyria itself but also the lands to the west which had for centuries been under Assyrian dominion.[^cite_note-Rollinger-26] Thus in the [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman) world both the [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) of Syria and the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Assyrian_Empire) of Mesopotamia (modern day [Iraq](7515928.md)) to the east were referred to as "Syrians" or "Syriacs", despite these being distinct peoples in their own right, a confusion which would continue into the modern world. Eventually parts of southern [Seleucid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) Syria were taken by the [Jewish](25955086.md) [Hasmonean dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasmonean_dynasty) upon the slow disintegration of the [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) Empire.&#x20;



Ancient city of [Palmyra](46709309.md) before the war



[Roman Theatre at Bosra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Theatre_at_Bosra) in the province of [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea), present-day Syria



[Temple of Jupiter, Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Jupiter,_Damascus)



[Apamea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apamea,_Syria), one of Syria's most prosperous cities in classical antiquity

Syria briefly came under [Armenian](387816.md) control from 83 BC, with the conquests of the Armenian king [Tigranes the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigranes_the_Great), who was welcomed as a savior from the Seleucids and Romans by the Syrian people. However, [Pompey the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey_the_Great), a general of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), rode to Syria and captured Antioch and turned Syria into a [Roman province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Syria) in 64 BC, thus ending Armenian control over the region which had lasted two decades. Syria prospered under Roman rule, being strategically located on the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road), which gave it massive wealth and importance, making it the battleground for the rivaling Romans and Persians. [Palmyra](46709309.md), a rich and sometimes powerful native Aramaic-speaking kingdom, arose in northern Syria in the 2nd century; the Palmyrene established a trade network that made the city one of the richest in the Roman Empire. In the late 3rd century the Palmyrene king [Odaenathus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odaenathus) defeated the Persian emperor [Shapur I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I) and controlled the entirety of the Roman East while his successor and widow [Zenobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenobia) established the [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire), which briefly conquered Egypt, Syria, Palestine, much of Asia Minor, Judah and Lebanon, before being finally brought under Roman control in 273.&#x20;

The northern Mesopotamian [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian) kingdom of [Adiabene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabene) controlled areas of north east Syria between 10 and 117, before it was conquered by Rome.[^cite_note-67] The Aramaic language has been found as far afield as [Hadrian's Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian%27s_Wall) in [Roman Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain),[^cite_note-68] with an inscription written by a Palmyrene emigrant at the site of Fort [Arbeia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbeia).[^cite_note-69] Control of Syria eventually passed from the Romans to the [Byzantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) with the split in the Roman Empire.[^cite_note-LibraryofCongress-43] The largely Aramaic-speaking population of Syria during the heyday of the Byzantine Empire was probably not exceeded again until the 19th century. Prior to the [Arab Islamic Conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) in the 7th century, the bulk of the population were Arameans, but Syria was also home to [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_people) and Roman ruling classes, Assyrians still dwelt in the north east, Phoenicians along the coasts, and Jewish and Armenian communities were also extant in major cities, with Nabateans and pre-Islamic [Arabs](2185.md) such as the [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids) and [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids) dwelling in the deserts of southern Syria. [Syriac Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Christianity) had taken hold as the major religion, although others still followed [Judaism](15624.md), [Mithraism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithraism), [Manicheanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manicheanism), [Greco-Roman Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_mythology), [Canaanite Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_Religion) and [Mesopotamian Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamian_Religion). Syria's large and prosperous population made Syria one of the most important of the Roman and Byzantine provinces, particularly during the 2nd and 3rd centuries AD.[^cite_note-Cavendish_Corporation_2006_183-70]&#x20;

Syrians held considerable power during the [Severan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severan_dynasty). The matriarch of the family and empress of Rome as wife of emperor [Septimius Severus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septimius_Severus) was [Julia Domna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Domna), a Syrian from the city of [Emesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesa) (modern day [Homs](77533.md)), [whose family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_family_of_Emesa) held hereditary rights to the priesthood of the god [El-Gabal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El-Gabal). Her great nephews, also Arabs from Syria, would also become Roman emperors, the first being [Elagabalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elagabalus) and the second his cousin [Alexander Severus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Severus). Another Roman emperor who was a Syrian was [Philip the Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_the_Arab) (Marcus Julius Philippus), who was born in [Roman Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea). He was emperor from 244 to 249[^cite_note-Cavendish_Corporation_2006_183-70] and ruled briefly during the [Crisis of the Third Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_of_the_Third_Century). During his reign, he focused on his home town of Philippopolis (modern day [Shahba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahba)) and began many construction projects to improve the city, most of which were halted after his death. Syria is significant in the [history of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity); Saul of Tarsus, better known as the [Apostle Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Paul), was [converted on the road to Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_of_Paul_the_Apostle) and emerged as a significant figure in the Christian Church at Antioch in ancient Syria.&#x20;

### Middle Ages



The [Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque) in Damascus built by Caliph [Al-Walid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Walid_I) followed the plan of the [Prophet's Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet%27s_Mosque) in Medina, and became the earliest example of a [congregational mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_mosque) that served as standard for future mosques

[^cite_note-\:33-71]

[Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad)'s first interaction with the people of Syria was during the [invasion of Dumatul Jandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Dumatul_Jandal) in July 626[^cite_note-autogenerated2-72] where he ordered his followers to invade Duma, because Muhammad received intelligence that some tribes there were involved in highway robbery and were preparing to attack [Medina](36636.md).[^cite_note-Mubarakpuri,_The_Sealed_Nectar_pp._193-194-73] [William Montgomery Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Montgomery_Watt) claims that this was the most significant expedition Muhammad ordered at the time, even though it received little notice in the primary sources. [Dumat Al-Jandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumat_Al-Jandal) was 800 kilometres (500 mi) from Medina, and Watt says that there was no immediate threat to Muhammad, other than the possibility that his communications to Syria and supplies to Medina would be interrupted. Watt says "It is tempting to suppose that Muhammad was already envisaging something of the expansion which took place after his death", and that the rapid march of his troops must have "impressed all those who heard of it".[^cite_note-Muhammad_at_Medina-74] [William Muir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Muir) also believes that the expedition was important as Muhammad followed by 1,000 men reached the confines of Syria, where distant tribes had learnt his name, while the political horizon of Muhammad was extended.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-72]



[Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) (green) with its capital in Damascus around 740

&#x20;By 640, Syria [was conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant) by the [Rashidun army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_army) led by [Khalid ibn al-Walid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_ibn_al-Walid). In the mid-7th century, the [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_dynasty) placed the capital of the empire in Damascus. The country's power declined during later Umayyad rule; this was mainly through totalitarianism, corruption and the resulting revolutions. The Umayyad dynasty was overthrown in 750 by the [Abbasid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_dynasty), which moved the capital of empire to [Baghdad](4492.md). [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) – made official under Umayyad rule[^cite_note-75] – became the dominant language, replacing [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic_language) of the Byzantine era. In 887, the Egypt-based [Tulunids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulunids) annexed Syria from the Abbasids and were later replaced by the Egypt-based [Ikhshidids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikhshidid_dynasty) and then by the [Hamdanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamdanid) originating in Aleppo founded by [Sayf al-Dawla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayf_al-Dawla).[^cite_note-76]



Umayyad [fresco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco) from [Qasr al-Hayr al-Gharbî](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr_al-Hayr_al-Gharb%C3%AE), built in the early 7th century

Sections of Syria were held by French, English, Italian and German overlords between 1098 and 1189 during the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) and were known collectively as the [Crusader states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusader_states), among which the primary one in Syria was the [Principality of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Antioch). The coastal mountainous region was occupied in part by the [Nizari Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizaris), the so-called [Assassins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Assassins), who had intermittent confrontations and truces with the Crusader States. Later in history when "the Nizaris faced renewed Frankish hostilities, they received timely assistance from the Ayyubids."[^cite_note-77] After a century of Seljuk rule, Syria was largely conquered (1175–1185) by the [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_people) liberator [Salah ad-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin), founder of the [Ayyubid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid) dynasty of Egypt. [Aleppo fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Aleppo_\(1260\)) to the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongols) of [Hulegu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulegu) in January 1260; Damascus fell in March, but then Hulegu was forced to break off his attack to return to China to deal with a succession dispute.&#x20;

A few months later, the [Mamluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk) arrived with an army from Egypt and defeated the Mongols in the [Battle of Ain Jalut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ain_Jalut) in [Galilee](12639.md). The Mamluk leader, [Baibars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baibars), made Damascus a provincial capital. When he died, power was taken by [Qalawun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qalawun). In the meantime, an emir named [Sunqur al-Ashqar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunqur_al-Ashqar) had tried to declare himself ruler of Damascus, but he was defeated by Qalawun on 21 June 1280 and fled to northern Syria. Al-Ashqar, who had married a Mongol woman, appealed for help from the Mongols. The Mongols of the [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) took Aleppo in October 1280, but Qalawun persuaded Al-Ashqar to join him, and they fought against the Mongols on 29 October 1281 in the [Second Battle of Homs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Homs), which was won by the Mamluks.[^cite_note-78] In 1400, the Muslim [Turco-Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition) conqueror [Tamurlane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamurlane) invaded Syria, in which he [sacked Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Aleppo_\(1400\))[^cite_note-79] and [captured Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Damascus_\(1400\)) after defeating the Mamluk army. The city's inhabitants were massacred, except for the artisans who were deported to [Samarkand](51491.md).[^cite_note-80] Tamurlane conducted massacres of the Assyrian Christian population, greatly reducing their numbers.[^cite_note-81] By the end of the 15th century, the discovery of a sea route from Europe to the [Far East](11672.md) ended the need for an [overland trade route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) through Syria.&#x20;

### Ottoman Syria

Main article: [Ottoman Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria)



[Tartus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartus) in Ottoman Syria, from an 1810 illustration by [Luigi Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luigi_Mayer)



"The Gate Of The Great [Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque), Damascus" by [Gustav Bauernfeind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Bauernfeind), 1890

In 1516, the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) invaded the [Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate), conquering Syria and incorporating it into its empire. The Ottoman system was not burdensome to Syrians because the Turks respected Arabic as the language of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and accepted the mantle of defenders of the faith. Damascus was made the major entrepot for [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), and as such it acquired a holy character to Muslims, because of the beneficial results of the countless pilgrims who passed through on the [hajj](23487999.md).[^cite_note-Syria_–_Ottoman_Empire-82]&#x20;

Ottoman administration followed a system that led to peaceful coexistence. Each ethno-religious minority—Arab [Shia Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Muslim), Arab [Sunni Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslim), [Syriac Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox), [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox), [Maronite Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maronite_Christians), [Assyrian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Christians), [Armenians](387816.md), [Kurds](17068.md) and [Jews](25955086.md)—constituted a [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)).[^cite_note-83] The religious heads of each community administered all personal status laws and performed certain civil functions as well.[^cite_note-Syria_–_Ottoman_Empire-82] In 1831, [Ibrahim Pasha of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Pasha_of_Egypt) renounced his loyalty to the empire and overran [Ottoman Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria), capturing Damascus. His short-term rule over the domain attempted to change the demographics and social structure of the region: he brought thousands of Egyptian villagers to populate the plains of [southern Syria](498693.md), rebuilt [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa) and settled it with veteran Egyptian soldiers aiming to turn it into a regional capital, and he crushed peasant and Druze rebellions and deported non-loyal tribesmen. By 1840, however, he had to surrender the area back to the Ottomans. From 1864, [Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) reforms were applied on Ottoman Syria, carving out the provinces (vilayets) of [Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleppo_Vilayet), [Zor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak_of_Zor), [Beirut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beirut_Vilayet) and [Damascus Vilayet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Vilayet); [Mutasarrifate of Mount Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutasarrifate_of_Mount_Lebanon) was created, and soon after the [Mutasarrifate of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutasarrifate_of_Jerusalem) was given a separate status.&#x20;



[Armenian](387816.md) deportees near Aleppo during the [Armenian genocide](140376.md), 1915

During [World War I](4764461.md), the Ottoman Empire entered the conflict as a [Central Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers). It ultimately suffered defeat and loss of control of the entire [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East) to the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) and [French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire). During the conflict, genocide against indigenous Christian peoples was carried out by the Ottomans and their allies in the form of the [Armenian genocide](140376.md) and [Assyrian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayfo), of which [Deir ez-Zor](1429146.md) in Ottoman Syria was the final destination of these death marches.[^cite_note-84] In the midst of World War I, two [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) diplomats (Frenchman [François Georges-Picot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Georges-Picot) and [Briton](19097669.md) [Mark Sykes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Sykes)) secretly agreed on the post-war division of the Ottoman Empire into respective zones of influence in the [Sykes-Picot Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes-Picot_Agreement) of 1916. Initially, the two territories were separated by a border that ran in an almost straight line from Jordan to Iran. However, the discovery of oil in the region of [Mosul](200475.md) just before the end of the war led to yet [another negotiation with France in 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1918_Clemenceau%E2%80%93Lloyd_George_Agreement_\(Middle_East\)) to cede this region to the British zone of influence, which was to become Iraq. The fate of the intermediate province of Zor was left unclear; its [occupation by Arab nationalists resulted in its attachment to Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Zor). This border was recognized internationally when Syria became a [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) mandate in 1920[^cite_note-85] and has not changed to date.&#x20;

### French Mandate

Main articles: [Mandate for Syria and the Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_for_Syria_and_the_Lebanon) and [Mandatory Syrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Syrian_Republic)



The inauguration of President [Hashim al-Atassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_al-Atassi) in 1936

In 1920, a short-lived independent [Kingdom of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Kingdom_of_Syria) was established under [Faisal I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq) of the [Hashemite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemites) family. However, his rule over Syria ended after only a few months, following the [Battle of Maysalun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Maysalun). French troops occupied Syria later that year after the [San Remo conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Remo_conference) proposed that the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) put Syria under a French mandate. General Gouraud had according to his secretary de Caix two options: "Either build a Syrian nation that does not exist... by smoothing the rifts which still divide it" or "cultivate and maintain all the phenomena, which require our arbitration that these divisions give". De Caix added "I must say only the second option interests me". This is what Gouraud did.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87]&#x20;

In 1925, [Sultan al-Atrash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_al-Atrash) led [a revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Syrian_Revolt) that broke out in the [Druze Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_al-Druze) and spread to engulf the whole of Syria and parts of Lebanon. Al-Atrash won several battles against the French, notably the [Battle of al-Kafr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_al-Kafr) on 21 July 1925, the [Battle of al-Mazraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_al-Mazraa) on 2–3 August 1925, and the battles of Salkhad, [al-Musayfirah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Musayfirah) and Sweida. France sent thousands of troops from Morocco and Senegal, leading the French to regain many cities, although resistance lasted until the spring of 1927. The French sentenced al-Atrash to death, but he had escaped with the rebels to Transjordan and was eventually pardoned. He returned to Syria in 1937 after the signing of the Syrian-French Treaty.&#x20;



Syrian rebels in [Ghouta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghouta) during the [Great Syrian Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Syrian_Revolt) against French colonial rule in the 1920s

Syria and France negotiated a [treaty of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Syrian_Treaty_of_Independence_\(1936\)) in September 1936, and [Hashim al-Atassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_al-Atassi) was the first president to be elected under the first incarnation of the modern republic of Syria. However, the treaty never came into force because the French Legislature refused to ratify it. With the fall of France in 1940 during [World War II](32927.md), Syria came under the control of [Vichy France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France) until the British and Free French occupied the country in the [Syria-Lebanon campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria-Lebanon_campaign) in July 1941. Continuing pressure from Syrian nationalists and the British [forced the French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant_Crisis) to evacuate their troops in April 1946, leaving the country in the hands of a republican government that had been formed during the mandate.[^cite_note-USDOS-88]&#x20;

### Independent Syrian Republic

Main article: [Second Syrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Syrian_Republic)

&#x20;Upheaval dominated Syrian politics from independence through the late 1960s. In May 1948, Syrian forces invaded [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)), together with other Arab states, and immediately [attacked Jewish settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_the_Kinarot_Valley).[^cite_note-89] President [Shukri al-Quwatli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shukri_al-Quwatli) instructed his troops in the front, "to destroy the Zionists".[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91] The invasion purpose was to prevent the establishment of the state of Israel.[^cite_note-Britan-92] Toward this end, the Syrian government engaged in an active process of recruiting former [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), including several former members of the [Schutzstaffel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schutzstaffel), to build up their armed forces and military intelligence capabilities.[^cite_note-93] Defeat in this war was one of several trigger factors for the [March 1949 Syrian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_1949_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) by Colonel [Husni al-Za'im](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husni_al-Za%27im), described as the first military overthrow of the [Arab World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World)[^cite_note-Britan-92] since the start of the Second World War. This was soon followed by another overthrow, by Colonel [Sami al-Hinnawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sami_al-Hinnawi), who was quickly deposed by Colonel [Adib Shishakli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adib_Shishakli), all within the same year.[^cite_note-Britan-92]



Aleppo in 1961

Shishakli would then later be overthrown in a [1954 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), restoring the previous parliamentary system.[^cite_note-Britan-92] However, by this time, power was increasingly concentrated in the military and security establishment.[^cite_note-Britan-92] The weakness of parliamentary institutions and the mismanagement of the economy led to unrest and the rising influence of [Nasserism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasserism) and other ideologies. There was fertile ground for various [Arab nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalist), [Syrian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_nationalism), and socialist movements, which represented disaffected elements of society. Notably included were religious minorities, who demanded radical reform.[^cite_note-Britan-92]&#x20;

In November 1956, as a direct result of the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis),[^cite_note-94] Syria signed a pact with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). This gave a foothold for communist influence within the government in exchange for military equipment.[^cite_note-Britan-92] [Turkey](11125639.md) then became worried about this increase in the strength of Syrian military technology, as it seemed feasible that Syria might attempt to retake [İskenderun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0skenderun). Only heated debates in the United Nations lessened the threat of war.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

### United Arab Republic

Main articles: [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic) and [1963 Syrian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat)



Gamal Abdel Nasser at [Aleppo](159244.md), 1960

On 1 February 1958, Syrian President [Shukri al-Quwatli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shukri_al-Quwatli) and Egypt's President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) announced the merging of Egypt and Syria, creating the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic), and all Syrian political parties, as well as the communists therein, ceased overt activities.[^cite_note-USDOS-88] Meanwhile, a group of Syrian Ba'athist officers, alarmed by the party's poor position and the increasing fragility of the union, decided to form a secret Military Committee; its initial members were Lieutenant-Colonel [Muhammad Umran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Umran), Major [Salah Jadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Jadid) and Captain Hafez al-Assad. Syria seceded from the union with Egypt on 28 September 1961, after a [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) and terminated the political union.&#x20;

#### Land reform

Agrarian reform measures were introduced which consisted of three interrelated programs: legislation regulating the relationship between agriculture laborers and landowners; legislation governing the ownership and use of private and state domain land and directing the economic organization of peasants; and measures reorganizing agricultural production under state control.[^cite_note-96] Despite high levels of inequality in land ownership these reforms allowed for more progress in redistribution of land from 1958 to 1961 than any other reforms in Syria's history, since independence.&#x20;

The first law passed (Law 134; passed 4 September 1958) was in response to concern about peasant mobilization and expanding peasants' rights.[^cite_note-Heydemann,_Steven_19992-97] This was designed to strengthen the position of sharecroppers and agricultural laborers in relation to land owners.[^cite_note-Heydemann,_Steven_19992-97] This law led to the creation of the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs, which announced the implementation of new laws that would allow the regulation of working condition especially for women and adolescents, set hours of work, and introduce the principle of minimum wage for paid laborers and an equitable division of harvest for sharecroppers.[^cite_note-98] Furthermore, it obligated landlords to honor both written and oral contracts, established collective bargaining, contained provisions for workers' compensation, health, housing, and employment services.[^cite_note-Heydemann,_Steven_19992-97] Law 134 was not designed strictly to protect workers. It also acknowledged the rights of landlords to form their own syndicates.[^cite_note-Heydemann,_Steven_19992-97]&#x20;

### Ba'athist Syria

Main article: [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md)

The instability which followed the [1961 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) culminated in the [8 March 1963 Ba'athist coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat). The takeover was engineered by members of the [Arab Socialist Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region), led by [Michel Aflaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Aflaq) and [Salah al-Din al-Bitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_al-Din_al-Bitar). The new Syrian cabinet was dominated by Ba'ath members.[^cite_note-USDOS-88][^cite_note-Britan-92] From the [1963 seizure of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) by its [Military Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Committee_\(Syria\)) to the [fall of the regime](78548066.md) in 2024, the Ba'ath party ruled Syria as a dictatorship which was frequently described as [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism),[^cite_note-tots-99] although some scholars rejected this description.[^cite_note-nt1-100][^cite_note-nt2-101] Ba'athists took control over country's politics, education, culture, religion and surveilled all aspects of civil society through its powerful *[Mukhabarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\))* (secret police). The [Syrian Arab Armed Forces](78567599.md) and secret police were integrated with the Ba'ath party apparatus; after the purging of traditional civilian and military elites by the regime.[^cite_note-102]&#x20;



The [Syrian Golan Heights](7515865.md) have been occupied by Israel since 1967

The 1963 Ba'athist coup marked a "radical break" in [modern Syrian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Syria#Modern_history), after which Ba'ath party monopolised power in the country to establish a [one-party state](102459.md) and shaped a socio-political order by enforcing its [state ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Ba%27athism).[^cite_note-103] On 23 February 1966, the neo-Ba'athist Military Committee carried out an [intra-party rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) against the Ba'athist Old Guard ([Aflaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Aflaq) and [Bitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_al-Din_al-Bitar)), imprisoned President [Amin al-Hafiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amin_al-Hafiz) and designated a [regionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutriyun), civilian Ba'ath government on 1 March.[^cite_note-Britan-92] Although [Nureddin al-Atassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nureddin_al-Atassi) became the formal head of state, [Salah Jadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Jadid) was Syria's effective ruler from 1966 until November 1970,[^cite_note-104] when he was deposed by [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), who at the time was Minister of Defense.[^cite_note-ps-105]&#x20;

&#x20;The coup led to the schism within the original pan-Arab Ba'ath Party: one [Iraqi-led ba'ath movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%27ath_Party_\(Iraqi-dominated_faction\)) (ruled Iraq from 1968 to 2003) and one [Syrian-led ba'ath movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%27ath_Party_\(Syrian-dominated_faction\)) was established. In the first half of 1967, a low-key state of war existed between Syria and Israel. Conflict over Israeli cultivation of land in the [demilitarized zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Syria_Mixed_Armistice_Commission) led to [7 April pre-war aerial clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_the_Six-Day_War#Israel_and_Syria) between Israel and Syria.[^cite_note-Tessler1994-106] When the [Six-Day War](29328.md) broke out between Egypt and Israel, Syria joined the war and attacked Israel as well. In the final days of the war, Israel turned its attention to Syria, capturing two-thirds of the [Golan Heights](7515865.md) in under 48 hours.[^cite_note-107] The defeat caused a split between Jadid and Assad over what steps to take next.[^cite_note-Khatib2012-108] Disagreement developed between Jadid, who controlled the party apparatus, and Assad, who controlled the military. The 1970 retreat of Syrian forces sent to aid the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) led by [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) during the "[Black September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September) (also known as the Jordan Civil War of 1970)" hostilities with Jordan reflected this disagreement.[^cite_note-109]



[Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), president of Syria from 1971 until [his death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Hafez_al-Assad) in 2000

The power struggle culminated in the November [1970 Syrian Corrective movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrective_Revolution_\(Syria\)), a bloodless military coup that installed Hafez al-Assad as the strongman of the government.[^cite_note-ps-105] Assad transformed a Ba'athist party state into a dictatorship marked by his pervasive grip on the party, [armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Armed_Forces), [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\)), media, education sector, religious and cultural spheres and all aspects of civil society. He assigned [Alawite](436616.md) loyalists to key posts in the military forces, bureaucracy, [intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\)) and the ruling elite. A cult of personality revolving around Hafez and his family became a core tenet of [Ba'athist ideology](36629941.md),[^cite_note-The_Sturdy_House_That_Assad_Built2-110] which espoused that [Assad dynasty](27334130.md) was destined to rule perennially.[^cite_note-111]&#x20;

On 6 October 1973, Syria and Egypt initiated the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War) against Israel. The [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md) reversed the initial Syrian gains and pushed deeper into Syrian territory.[^cite_note-112] The village of [Quneitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra) was largely destroyed by the Israeli army.&#x20;

In the late 1970s, an [Islamist uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_uprising_in_Syria) by the [Muslim Brotherhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Syria) was aimed against the government. Islamists attacked civilians and off-duty military personnel, leading security forces to also kill civilians in retaliatory strikes. The uprising had reached its climax in the 1982 [Hama massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Hama_massacre),[^cite_note-113] when more than 2,000 to 25,000[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarréMichaud1983159-114]-40,000 people, including civilians, were killed by [Ba'athist Syrian](78567599.md) troops and allied paramilitaries.[^cite_note-wright2008-115][^cite_note-Amos-116] It has been described as the "single deadliest act" of violence perpetrated by any state upon its own population in [modern Arab history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Middle_East).[^cite_note-wright2008-115][^cite_note-Amos-116]&#x20;

In a major shift in relations with both other [Arab states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) and the Western world, Syria participated in the United States-led [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) against [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein). The country participated in the multilateral [Madrid Conference of 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Conference_of_1991), and during the 1990s engaged in negotiations with Israel along with Palestine and Jordan. These negotiations failed, and there have been no further direct Syrian-Israeli talks since Assad's meeting with U.S. President [Bill Clinton](3356.md) in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva) in 2000.[^cite_note-117]&#x20;

#### Involvement of Lebanese war

Main article: [Syrian occupation of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon)

Hafez al-Assad died on 10 June 2000. His son, Bashar al-Assad, was elected president in [an election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_presidential_election,_2000) in which he ran unopposed.[^cite_note-USDOS-88] His election saw the birth of the [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring) and hopes of reform, but by autumn 2001, his government had suppressed the movement, imprisoning some of its leading intellectuals.[^cite_note-Alan-118] Instead, reforms were limited to some market reforms.[^cite_note-The_Sturdy_House_That_Assad_Built2-110][^cite_note-119]&#x20;

On 5 October 2003 Israel [bombed a site near Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ain_es_Saheb_airstrike), claiming it was a terrorist training facility for members of [Islamic Jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Jihad_Movement_in_Palestine).[^cite_note-120] In March 2004, [Syrian Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Kurds) and Arabs [clashed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Al-Qamishli_riots) in [al-Qamishli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qamishli). Signs of rioting were seen in Qamishli and [Hasakeh](7573335.md).[^cite_note-121] In 2005, Syria ended its military presence in Lebanon.[^cite_note-122] The [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rafic_Hariri) of [Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafic_Hariri) in 2005 led to international condemnation and triggered [protests in Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Revolution), which forced the [Assad regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assad_regime) to end its 29-year-old [military occupation in Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon).[^cite_note-The_Los_Angeles_Times-123] On 6 September 2007, foreign jet fighters, suspected as Israeli, reportedly carried out [Operation Orchard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Orchard) against a suspected nuclear reactor under construction by North Korean technicians.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

#### Syrian revolution and civil war

Main articles: [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) and [Syrian civil war](30741795.md)



Military situation in December 2015. [Islamic State](9087364.md)-[controlled territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_the_Islamic_State) is in grey.

Nepotism of ruling elites and [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) regime caused [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) as part of the wider [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring). Public demonstrations across Syria began on 26 January 2011 and developed into a nationwide uprising. Protesters demanded the resignation of Assad, the overthrow of his government, and an end to nearly five decades of Ba'ath Party rule. Beginning in spring 2011, the Syrian government deployed the Syrian Army to quell the uprising, and several cities were besieged,[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-http-126] though the unrest continued. According to some witnesses, soldiers, who refused to open fire on civilians, were summarily executed by the Syrian Army.[^cite_note-defect-127] The Syrian government denied reports of defections, and blamed armed gangs for causing trouble.[^cite_note-crackdown-128] In early autumn 2011, civilians and army defectors began forming fighting units, which began an [insurgency campaign](30741795.md) against the Syrian Army. The insurgents unified under the banner of the [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army) and fought in an increasingly organized fashion; however, the civilian component of the armed opposition lacked an organized leadership.[^cite_note-\:2-129]&#x20;



Pro-Assad demonstration in the capital Damascus after [US-led missile strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2018_missile_strikes_against_Syria) in April 2018

The [Arab League](52625.md), the United States, the European Union states, the [Gulf Cooperation Council states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperation_Council_for_the_Arab_States_of_the_Gulf), and other countries have condemned the use of violence against the protesters.[^cite_note-\:2-129] China and Russia have avoided condemning the government or applying sanctions, saying that such methods could escalate into foreign intervention. However, military intervention has been ruled out by most countries.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-\:3-132] The Arab League suspended Syria's membership over the government's response to the crisis,[^cite_note-nytimes-suspend-133] but sent an [observer mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_League_Monitors_in_Syria) in December 2011, as part of its [proposal for peaceful resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_conflict_peace_proposals) of the crisis.[^cite_note-\:3-132]&#x20;

By 2024, the Syrian civil war had resulted in more than 600,000 deaths,[^cite_note-134] with pro-Assad forces causing more than 90% of the [total civilian casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Syrian_civil_war).[^cite_note-143] Between March 2011 and March 2021, more than 306,000 civilians were killed in the civil war.**[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145]** The war led to a [massive refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war), with an estimated 7.6 million [internally displaced people](477535.md) (July 2015 [UNHCR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNHCR) figure) and over 5 million refugees (July 2017 registered by [UNHCR](31964.md)).[^cite_note-UNHCR-146] The war has also worsened economic conditions, with more than 90% of the population living in poverty and 80% facing food insecurity.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-153]&#x20;

#### Rise and fall of Islamic State and factional escalation

Main articles: [War against the Islamic State](43517689.md) and [Foreign involvement in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)



IS's capital [Raqqa](858965.md) suffered extensive damage during the [battle of Raqqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Raqqa_\(2017\)) in June–October 2017

Taking advantage of the Syrian civil war, the [Islamic State of Iraq and Syria](9087364.md) (ISIS) won many battles against the rebel factions and the Ba'athist-led Syrian government in 2014. ISIS was able to seize control of large parts of Eastern Syria, prompting a United States-led coalition to launch an [aerial bombing campaign](43517689.md) against ISIS, while providing [ground support](43683196.md) and supplies to the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) (SDF), a Kurdish-dominated coalition led by the [People's Defense Units](36497032.md) (YPG).&#x20;

In August 2016, Turkey launched a [multi-pronged invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) of northern Syria, in response to the creation of the Kurdish-led [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria), also known as Rojava, while also fighting ISIS and government forces in the process. During this time, Turkey helped establish the [Syrian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) (SNA) out of remnants of the Free Syrian Army. [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) and later Russia also [intervened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) in the civil war on behalf of the Assad government to help defeat ISIS and attack both SDF and SNA. The ISIS eventually lost its territory after the [fall of IS in Mosul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Mosul) and increasingly resorted to more terror bombings and insurgency operations that continues in present.&#x20;

#### Rebel offensive and fall of the Ba'athist regime

Main articles: [2024 Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md) and [Fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md)



Military situation before the [opposition offensives](78460726.md) in late 2024.\
Territories controlled by the [SDF](48205248.md) and the [United States](3434750.md) (yellow), [IS](9087364.md) (grey), the [Syrian Arab Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Armed_Forces), [Russia](25391.md) and [Iran](14653.md) (red), [SNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) and [Turkey](11125639.md) (light green), [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (white), [SFA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Commando_Army) and the [United States](3434750.md) (teal), [Israeli-occupied Golan Heights](60364960.md) (not shown as part of Syria) .

In December 2024, violence flared up once again. Rebel factions, led by the Islamist group [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (HTS), [took control of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2024\)) in a lightning offensive, prompting a retaliatory airstrike campaign by Syrian regime forces, supported by [Russian aviation assets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Aerospace_Forces). The strikes, which targeted population centers and several hospitals in [rebel-held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idlib_insurgency) city of [Idlib](2528679.md), resulted in at least 25 deaths according to the White Helmets rescue group. NATO issued a joint statement calling for the protection of civilians and critical infrastructure to prevent further displacement and ensure humanitarian access. They stressed the urgent need for a Syrian-led political solution, in accordance with UN Security Council Resolution 2254, which advocates for dialogue between the Syrian government and opposition forces.&#x20;

The [rebel offensive](78460726.md), which had begun on 27 November, continued its [advance into Hama province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Hama_offensive) following their capture of Aleppo.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] On 30 November, Brigadier General [Hossam Louka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossam_Louka), director of the [General Security Directorate](19204147.md), attempted to stage a coup in Damascus while Bashar al-Assad was in Moscow, but would be foiled by Bashar's younger brother [Maher](3139640.md).[^cite_note-157] On 4 December, fierce clashes erupted in Hama province as the Syrian army engaged rebel forces in a bid to halt their advance on the key city of [Hama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hama). Government forces claimed to have launched a counteroffensive with air support, pushing back rebel factions, including HTS, around six miles from the city. However, despite reinforcements, the rebels captured the city on 5 December.[^cite_note-158] The fighting led to widespread displacement, with nearly 50,000 people fleeing the area and over 600 casualties reported, including 104 civilians.[^cite_note-159]&#x20;



Military situation after the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in December 2024.\
Territories controlled by the [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (white) and [SOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Operations_Room) (pink), [Turkey](11125639.md) and [SNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) (light green), the [United States](3434750.md) and [SDF](48205248.md) (yellow), [IS](9087364.md) (grey), the [Syrian uncertain/mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian) (red/light grey), the [United States](3434750.md) and [SFA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Commando_Army) (teal), [Israeli occupation](78549398.md) (blue).

Rebel forces reached the outskirts of [Homs](77533.md) on 5 December, beginning a [three-day battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Homs_offensive) for the city. Simultaneously, an HTS-coordinated[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-\:32-161] [mass uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Syria_offensive_\(2024\)) led by a [coalition of Druze tribes and opposition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Operations_Room) captured the southern cities of [Sweida](2590570.md) and [Daraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daraa_insurgency) by 6 December,[^cite_note-162] and rapidly advanced northwards to encircle Damascus over the following day.[^cite_note-reuters-homs-163][^cite_note-164] Homs was captured by rebel forces by the early morning of 8 December, leaving no major regime strongholds between the rebel advance and Damascus itself.[^cite_note-Captured-165]&#x20;

Cut off from the [Alawite](436616.md) heartland of [Tartus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartus_Governorate) and [Latakia](7445289.md) governorates, faced with a rebel pincer from both north and south bearing down on Damascus, and with no hope of foreign intervention from the regime's Russian and Iranian benefactors, Assadist authority over remaining regime-held territories rapidly disintegrated.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-reuters-167] The [Syrian Arab Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Armed_Forces) melted away as its soldiers abandoned their weapons and uniforms,[^cite_note-guard2024-168] many [deserting](488824.md) across the border to [Iraq](7515928.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md). Opposition forces [captured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)) the capital Damascus on 8 December, toppling Bashar al-Assad's government and ending the Assad family's 53-year-long rule over the country.[^cite_note-reuters-homs-163] Assad fled to [Moscow](19004.md) with his family, where he was granted asylum.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170]&#x20;

### Transitional period



Syrian revolutionaries toppling a statue of [Bassel al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bassel_al-Assad) in [New Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Aleppo), 30 November 2024

Following the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md), Assad's ninth prime minister [Mohammad Ghazi al-Jalali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ghazi_al-Jalali), with support from the opposition and [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md), remained at his post in a caretaker capacity until a [caretaker government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government) led by [Mohammed al-Bashir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_al-Bashir) was formed the following day.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-ilkha.com-172] Al-Jalali called for fresh elections so that the Syrian people may choose their new leaders.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-reuters-homs-163]&#x20;

Prior to the fall of the Assad regime, Mohammed al-Bashir headed the [Syrian Salvation Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Salvation_Government) (SSG) formed in the province of [Idlib](2528679.md) by [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (HTS), the Islamist militant organization which led the overthrow of Assad in December 2024. In general, the formation of the Transitional Government was scaling of the SSG "to the whole of Syria", as the composition of the new government was almost the same as that of the SSG.
