### Ethnic groups

Main articles: [Syrians](8862873.md) and [Ethnic groups in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Syria)



Damascus traditional clothing

Syrians are an overall indigenous [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) people, closely related to their immediate neighbors, such as [Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people), [Palestinians](23267.md), [Jordanians](15717.md) and [Jews](25955086.md).[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] Syria has a population of approximately 26,000,000 (2025 estimate). [Syrian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_people), together with some 600,000 [Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinians_in_Syria) not including the 6 million refugees outside the country make up roughly 74% of the population.[^cite_note-CIA-3] The indigenous [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrians_in_Syria) and [Western Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Neo-Aramaic)-speakers number around 400,000 people,[^cite_note-366] with the Western Aramaic-speakers living mainly in the villages of [Maaloula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maaloula), [Jubb'adin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jubb%27adin) and [Bakh'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Sarkha_\(Bakhah\)), while the Assyrians mainly reside in the north and northeast (Homs, Aleppo, Qamishli, Hasakah). Many (particularly the Assyrian group) still retain several [Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Aramaic) dialects as spoken and written languages.[^cite_note-367]&#x20;

The second-largest ethnic group in Syria are the [Kurds](3821855.md). They constitute about 9%[^cite_note-SyriaKurds-368] to 10%[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] of the population, or approximately 2 million people (including 40,000 [Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidis_in_Syria)[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10]). Most Kurds reside in the northeastern corner of Syria and most speak the [Kurmanji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurmanji) variant of the [Kurdish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_language).[^cite_note-SyriaKurds-368] The third largest ethnic group are the [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language)-speaking [Syrian Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Turkmen)/Turkoman. There are no reliable estimates of their total population, with estimates ranging from several hundred thousand to 3.5 million.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-Turkmenistan-371] The fourth largest ethnic group are the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian-Assyrians) (3–4%),[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] followed by the [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Syria) (1.5%)[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] and the [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Syria) (1%),[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] most of which are the descendants of refugees who arrived in Syria during the [Armenian genocide](140376.md). Syria holds the [7th largest Armenian population in the world](214413.md). They are mainly gathered in Aleppo, [Qamishli](524369.md), Damascus and [Kesab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesab).&#x20;



The ethno-religious composition of Syria

There are also smaller ethnic minority groups, such as the [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians), [Bosnians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnians), [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians), [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Syria), [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians), [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) and [Russians](59510.md).[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] However, most of these ethnic minorities have become [Arabized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabized) to some degree, particularly those who practice the [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) faith.[^cite_note-Khalifa2013-10] The largest concentration of the [Syrian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_diaspora) outside the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) is in [Brazil](3383.md), which has millions of people of Arab and other Near Eastern ancestries.[^cite_note-372] Brazil is the first country in the Americas to offer humanitarian visas to Syrian refugees.[^cite_note-373] The majority of [Arab Argentines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Argentine) are from either Lebanese or Syrian background.[^cite_note-374]&#x20;

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Syria)

[Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic) is the [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) of the country.[^cite_note-CoSAR-375] Several modern [Arabic dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Arabic) are used in everyday life, most notably [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic) in the west and [Mesopotamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamian_Arabic) in the northeast. According to *The Encyclopedia of Arabic Language and Linguistics*, in addition to Arabic, the following languages are spoken in the country, in order of the number of speakers: [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_language) (recognized in 2026 as a [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language) of Syria),[^cite_note-al-monitor2026-01-16-5][^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language),[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] [Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Aramaic_languages) (four dialects),[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] [Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassian_languages),[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] [Chechen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechen_language),[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language),[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] and finally [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language).[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376] Besides Kurdish, none of these minority languages have official status.[^cite_note-Behnstedt-376]&#x20;

Many educated Syrians speak [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language), and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) (especially among the bourgeoisie and the [Christian community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Syria); there are fewer than 4,500 [French speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_distribution_of_French_speakers) today), but English is more widely understood (between 650,000 and one million speakers, as a second language). Before the start of the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), around 8,000 students were enrolled in French-speaking Catholic schools.[^cite_note-377]&#x20;

[Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic) was the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) of the region before the advent of [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic), and is still spoken among [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), and [Classical Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) is still used as the liturgical language of [various Syriac Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Christianity). Most remarkably, [Western Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Neo-Aramaic) is still spoken in the village of [Maaloula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maaloula) as well as two neighboring villages, 56 km (35 mi) northeast of Damascus. English and French are widely spoken as second languages, but English is more often used.[^cite_note-378]&#x20;

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Syria), [Islam in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Syria), and [Christianity in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Syria)



[Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque)

[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Syria) is the largest and predominant religion in Syria, comprising 87% of the population. [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) make up around 74% of the population[^cite_note-CIA-3] and Sunni Arabs account for 59–60%. Most Kurds (8.5%)[^cite_note-Drysdale\&Hinnebusch1991-379] and most Turkmens (3%)[^cite_note-Drysdale\&Hinnebusch1991-379] are Sunni, while 3% of Syrians are [Shia Muslims](26961.md) (particularly [Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailism), and [Twelvers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi%27ism) but there are also Arabs, Kurds and Turkmens), 10% are [Alawites](436616.md), 10% are [Christians](18337522.md)[^cite_note-CIA-3] (the majority are Antiochian Greek Orthodox, the rest are Syriac Orthodox, Greek Catholic and other Catholic Rites, Armenian Orthodox, Assyrian Church of the East, Protestants and other denominations), and 3% [Druzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze).[^cite_note-CIA-3] Druze number around 500,000 and concentrate mainly in the southern area of [Jabal al-Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_al-Druze).[^cite_note-book-380] According to the [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives) (ARDA), 94.17% of Syrians are Muslims–79.19% are Sunnis and 14.10% are Shias (including Alawites)–and 3.84% of Syrians are Christians as of 2020.[^cite_note-\:4-12]&#x20;

Because the [Assad family](27334130.md) is Alawite, Alawites have historically dominated key government and military positions.[^cite_note-The_Sturdy_House_That_Assad_Built2-110][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382]&#x20;

Christians numbering 1.2 million, a sizable number of whom are found among Syria's population of Palestinian and Iraqi refugees, are divided into several denominations. The [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Antioch) makes up 45.7% of the Christian population in Syria, whilst [Syriac Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church) make up 22.4%; the [Armenian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church) make up 10.9% and Catholicism (including [Greek Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melkite_Greek_Catholic_Church), [Syriac Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Catholic_Church), [Armenian Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Catholic_Church), [Maronite](66391078.md), [Chaldean Catholic](698821.md) and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_liturgical_rites)) make up 16.2%; [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East) and several smaller Christian denominations account for the remainder. Many Christian [monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Monasteries_in_Syria) also exist. Many Christian Syrians belong to a high socio-economic class.[^cite_note-383] As per one estimate, the count of Christians affiliated with established denominations in Syria has dropped from approximately 2.5 million prior to the civil war, to about 500,000 in 2023.[^cite_note-384]&#x20;

Syria was once home to a substantial population of [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Syria), with large communities in Damascus, Aleppo, and [Qamishli](524369.md).[^cite_note-\:5-385] Due to a combination of persecution in Syria and opportunities elsewhere, the Jews began to emigrate in the second half of the 19th century to Great Britain, the United States, and Israel.[^cite_note-\:5-385] The process was completed with the establishment of Israel in 1948.[^cite_note-\:5-385] The remaining Jewish population dwindled as a result of the civil war. Today 100 Jews live in Syria.[^cite_note-\:5-385] The United States is home to a large Syrian Jewish community, which is still considered as Syrian citizens by the Syrian government.&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Syria)



Faculty of Arts and Humanities in [Aleppo University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleppo_University)

Education is free and compulsory from ages 6 to 12. Schooling consists of six years of primary education followed by a three-year general or [vocational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocation) training period and a three-year academic or vocational program. The second three-year period of academic training is required for university [admission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_and_college_admission). Total enrollment at [post-secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) schools is over 150,000. The [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy) rate of Syrians aged 15 and older is 90.7% for males and 82.2% for females.[^cite_note-USDOS-88][^cite_note-impact-se-386]&#x20;



[UIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Institute_for_Statistics) adult literacy rate of Syria

There are ten public universities in Syria and 39 private universities.[^cite_note-387] The top two state universities are [Damascus University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_University) (210,000 students as of 2014)[^cite_note-388] and [University of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Aleppo).[^cite_note-389] The top private universities in Syria are: [Syrian Private University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Private_University), [Arab International University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_International_University), [University of Kalamoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Kalamoon) and [International University for Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_University_for_Science_and_Technology). There are also many higher institutes in Syria, like the [Higher Institute of Business Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_Institute_of_Business_Administration), which offer undergraduate and graduate programs in business.[^cite_note-390]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Syria)

The [Syrian civil war](30741795.md) profoundly impacted the nation's health demographics and infrastructure.[^cite_note-\:8-391] As of 2023, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) estimated Syria's population at approximately 23.6 million.[^cite_note-\:9-392]&#x20;

In 2021, the average life expectancy in Syria was 73.1 years.[^cite_note-\:9-392] The infant mortality rate has seen a decline over recent years, with estimates indicating 12.1 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2022.[^cite_note-393] The under-five mortality rate was reported at 21.4 deaths per 1,000 live births.[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

Prior to the conflict, Syria had a maternal mortality ratio of 52 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2009.[^cite_note-\:8-391] However, the war disrupted maternal healthcare services, leading to increased risks for pregnant women.&#x20;

The protracted conflict severely damaged Syria's healthcare infrastructure. In 2023 over half of the country's hospitals were non-functional, and approximately 15.3 million people were in need of health support, representing 72% of the population. This marked a 25% increase from 2022, highlighting the escalating health crisis.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-\:10-396]&#x20;

Non-communicable diseases, particularly [cardiovascular conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease), were leading causes of death before the conflict. However, during the war, conflict-related injuries and deaths surged, significantly altering the health landscape.[^cite_note-\:8-391][^cite_note-\:10-396]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Syria)



[Dabke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabke) combines circle dance and line dancing and is widely performed at weddings and other joyous occasions.

Syria is a traditional society with a long cultural history.[^cite_note-397] Importance is placed on family, religion, education, self-discipline and respect. Syrians' taste for the traditional arts is expressed in dances such as the al-Samah, the [Dabkeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabkeh) in all their variations, and the sword dance. Marriage ceremonies and the births of children are occasions for the lively demonstration of folk customs.[^cite_note-traditional-398]&#x20;

### Cuisine

[Syrian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_cuisine) is rich and varied in its ingredients, linked to the regions where a specific dish has originated. Syrian food mostly consists of southern Mediterranean, Greek, and Southwest Asian dishes. Some Syrian dishes also evolved from Turkish and French cooking: dishes like [shish kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab), stuffed zucchini/courgette, and *[yabraʾ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma)* (stuffed grape leaves, the word *yabraʾ* deriving from the Turkish word *yaprak*, meaning leaf).&#x20;

The main dishes are [kibbeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kibbeh), [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus), [tabbouleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabbouleh), [fattoush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fattoush), [labneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strained_yogurt), [shawarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawarma), [mujaddara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujaddara), [shanklish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanklish), [pastırma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Past%C4%B1rma), [sujuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sujuk) and baklava. [Baklava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baklava) is made of [filo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filo) pastry filled with chopped nuts and soaked in [honey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honey). Syrians often serve selections of appetizers, known as [meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze), before the main course. [Za'atar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Za%27atar), [minced beef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minced_beef), and cheese [manakish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manakish) are popular [hors d'œuvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hors_d%27%C5%93uvres). The Arabic flatbread khubz is always eaten together with meze.&#x20;

Drinks vary, depending on the time of day and the occasion. [Arabic coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_coffee) is the most well-known hot drink, usually prepared in the morning at breakfast or in the evening. It is usually served for guests or after food. [Arak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arak_\(distilled_beverage\)), an alcoholic drink, is a well-known beverage, served mostly on special occasions. Other Syrian beverages include [ayran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayran), [jallab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jallab), [white coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_coffee), and a locally manufactured beer called Al Shark.[^cite_note-399]&#x20;

### Literature



Syrian poet, essayist and translator [Adunis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adunis)

The [literature of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_literature) has contributed to [Arabic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_literature) and has a proud tradition of oral and written poetry. Syrian writers, many of whom migrated to Egypt, played a crucial role in the [nahda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahda) or Arab literary and cultural revival of the 19th century. Prominent contemporary Syrian writers include, among others, [Adonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Ahmad_Said), [Muhammad Maghout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Maghut), [Haidar Haidar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haidar_Haidar), [Ghada al-Samman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghada_al-Samman), [Nizar Qabbani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizar_Qabbani) and [Zakariyya Tamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakariyya_Tamer).&#x20;

Ba'ath Party rule brought about renewed censorship.[^cite_note-hrw-2019-293][^cite_note-ohchr-2020-294] In this context, the genre of the historical novel, spearheaded by Nabil Sulayman, [Fawwaz Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fawwaz_Haddad), Khyri al-Dhahabi and Nihad Siris, is sometimes used as a means of expressing dissent, critiquing the present through a depiction of the past. Syrian [folk narrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_folklores), as a subgenre of historical fiction, is imbued with [magical realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magical_realism), and is also used as a means of veiled criticism of the present. [Salim Barakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salim_Barakat), a Syrian émigré living in Sweden, is one of the leading figures of the genre. Contemporary Syrian literature also encompasses science fiction and futuristic [utopiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utopia) (Nuhad Sharif, Talib Umran), which may also serve as media of dissent.&#x20;

### Music

The [Syrian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Syria) scene, in particular that of Damascus, has long been among the Arab world's most important, especially in the field of [classical Arab music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arab_music). Syria has produced several pan-Arab stars, including [Asmahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asmahan), [Farid al-Atrash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farid_al-Atrash) and singer [Lena Chamamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lena_Chamamyan). The city of Aleppo is known for its [muwashshah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muwashshah), a form of [Andalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andalous) sung poetry popularized by [Sabri Moudallal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabri_Moudallal), as well as for popular stars like [Sabah Fakhri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Fakhri).&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Media of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Syria)



[Suzan Najm Aldeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzan_Najm_Aldeen), Syrian actress

[Television was introduced to Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Syria) and Egypt in 1960, when both were part of the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic). It broadcast in black and white until 1976.[^cite_note-\:6-400] [Syrian soap operas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_soap_operas) have considerable market penetration throughout the eastern Arab world.[^cite_note-\:6-400]&#x20;

During Ba'athist Syria, nearly all of Syria's media outlets were state-owned.[^cite_note-401] The authorities operated several intelligence agencies,[^cite_note-402] among them [Shu'bat al-Mukhabarat al-'Askariyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_\(Syria\)), employing many operatives.[^cite_note-403] During the civil war many of Syria's artists, poets, writers and activists had been incarcerated, and some have been killed, including famed cartoonist [Akram Raslan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akram_Raslam).[^cite_note-404]&#x20;

More than a year after Assad's fall, Syria's media landscape has opened considerably, with state outlets being restructured and exiled and opposition media returning. The authorities are preparing a new media law, while press freedom groups are calling for independent regulation, protection of sources and safeguards against the detention of journalists. Political instability, weak legal frameworks, poor infrastructure, limited funding and low public trust remain major challenges.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]&#x20;

    &#x20;**Kurds** are the second largest ethnic group in Syria, making up around 10% of the Syrian population and distributed among four regions...with a Yazidi minority that numbers around 40,000...\
    **Turkmen** are the third-largest ethnic group in Syria, making up around 4–5% of the population. Some estimations indicate that they are the second biggest group, outnumbering Kurds, drawing on the fact that Turkmen are divided into two groups: the rural Turkmen who make up 30% of the Turkmen in Syria and have kept their mother tongue, and the urban Turkmen who have become Arabised and no longer speak their mother language...\
    &#x20;**Assyrians** are the fourth-largest ethnic group in Syria. They represent the original and oldest inhabitants of Syria, today making up around 3–4% of the Syrian population...\
    &#x20;**Circassians** are the fifth-largest ethnic group in Syria, making up around 1.5% of the population...\
    &#x20;**Armenians** are sixth-largest ethnic group in Syria, making up around 1% of the population...\
    &#x20;There are also a small number of other ethnic groups in Syria, including Greeks, Persians, Albanians, Bosnian, Pashtuns, Russians and Georgians...
